## [Not sold on Tesla Powerwall? There are great battery alternatives out there \[update\]](https://electrek.co/2026/11/28/not-sold-on-tesla-powerwall-there-are-some-great-home-battery-alternatives-out-there/)

via Electrek Feed · 2026-11-28

![](https://electrek.co/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2026/07/home-solar-battery.png?w=1600)

[Between increasingly extreme weather](https://electrek.co/2026/07/15/new-normal-the-us-has-suffered-a-major-power-outage-every-month-of-2026/), rising electricity demands, and the rapid expansion of data centers, energy independence is starting to look less like a talking point for doomsday preppers and more like sound planning. That shift in thinking is driving more homeowners than ever towards solar-plus-battery energy solutions like the industry-leading Tesla Powerwall – but Powerwall isn’t the only game in town, anymore.

**UPDATE 28NOV26**: added the new Briggs & Stratton 16 kWh SimpliPHI.

## [Marketplace Holiday Special](https://www.marketplace.org/episode/2026/11/28/marketplace-holiday-special)

via Market Place · 2026-11-28

In this holiday special, host Kristen Schwab explores the business and economics of food, from a budget-friendly shopping trip at a Tennessee Walmart to a youth-run urban farm on Chicago's South Side. We also meet a Native fishing family keeping tribal salmon traditions alive on the Columbia River; we go hunting in Maryland; and we visit Afghan refugees building a restaurant scene in Houston. Kristen also heads home to Minnesota to learn her mom's famous teriyaki chicken, which she makes without a recipe.

## [Ukraine war rattles farmers, puts world food supply at risk](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/69i1v799a3em2kcytkiqestpsexkg3)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-28, updated 2026-11-27

In the past week, Ukraine blew up a [Russian warship docked in Libya](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/zelenskiy-says-ukraine-sank-russian-corvette-near-libyas-coast-hit-cargo-ship-2026-11-23/) and hit a [Russian cargo ship with drones](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/zelenskiy-says-ukraine-sank-russian-corvette-near-libyas-coast-hit-cargo-ship-2026-11-23/) in the Gulf of Finland, escalating and extending their war with Russia into the Baltic and Mediterranean seas.

President Trump, who once [boasted](https://apnews.com/article/trump-russia-ukraine-war-un-election-a78ecb843af452b8dda1d52d137ca893) that he could end the war in one day, now stands helplessly by as the fighting rages and [hundreds of thousands of troops have died](https://www.csis.org/analysis/russian-blood-and-treasure-ballooning-costs-putins-war).

It’s tragic … and it’s inevitable that the cost of the war in blood for the combatants also carries costs that are measured in terms of human suffering elsewhere.

There are no innocent bystanders.

The [Council on Foreign Relations](https://www.cfr.org/articles/hunger-on-the-horizon-the-new-black-sea-grain-crisis) warns that “ _recent escalation in the Russia-Ukraine war has halted the Black Sea grain trade and metastasized into a war on the global food system. Combined with drought in the Northern Hemisphere, a looming super El Niño, and persistent instability in the Middle East—the world is on the verge of a crisis that could erase years of progress in reducing global hunger and push millions into acute food insecurity.”_

Here in South Dakota, our entire ag economy is standing by and waiting for the ramifications that will materialize if fighting in the seas of Europe turns crucial shipping lanes into bottlenecks that hamper grain and fertilizer shipments from the combatants.

The stakes are huge.

[Ukraine](https://www.csis.org/analysis/russian-blood-and-treasure-ballooning-costs-putins-war) ranks #5 among the world’s wheat exporters, accounting for 9% of global exports. The country’s rankings for corn are #4 and 12%, respectively.

[Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_grain_exports) is the world’s leading wheat exporter. It too has historically used its outlets into the Black Sea to ship grain, but since the war with Ukraine has put that route at risk, Russia has been [moving its grain through northern ports](https://www.ft.com/content/50aeab56-c5e3-44c5-9117-8fadc2f980fb?syn-25a6b1a6=1#:~:text=Russia%20is%20testing%20exporting%20grain,route%20via%20the%20Black%20Sea.). Last week’s Ukrainian drone strike to the north, in the Gulf of Finland, [may be an indication that Ukraine can interfere](https://www.themoscowtimes.com/2026/11/26/ukraine-has-shaken-russia-s-confidence-in-its-naval-power-projection-a812382) with Russian shipments from the north.

And it’s not just grain shipments that are at risk. Fertilizer is a big part of this picture.

The inevitable increase in grain prices should a major escalation of the war materialize would be [offset by a surge in fertilizer prices](https://www.fb.org/intel/markets/hormuz-price-shocks-outpace-higher-crop-prices), which have [already been inflated](https://farmdocdaily.illinois.edu/2026/05/fertilizer-cost-increases-resulting-from-the-iran-conflict.html) by the U.S. war against Iran. Up to now, Russia, being the [world’s largest exporter of fertilizer](https://carnegieendowment.org/russia-eurasia/politika/2026/03/russia-new-fertilizer-export), has been able to shift much of its fertilizer exports to its northern ports, but Ukraine’s attack on that cargo ship in the Baltic Sea is effectively a statement that shipping from those ports could be endangered.

This thing could get messier. Farmers, who should be prepared for anything, can find [hedging mechanisms](https://www.ers.usda.gov/topics/farm-practices-management/risk-management/risk-management-strategies) that will take substantial risk out of market swings as the geo-political news ebbs and flows along with the bloody tides of the Baltic, the Black and now the Mediterranean seas.

_John Tsitrian is a businessman and writer from the Black Hills. He was a weekly columnist for the Rapid City Journal for 20 years. His articles and commentary have also appeared in The Los Angeles Times, The Denver Post and The Omaha World-Herald. Tsitrian served in the Marines for three years (1966-69), including a 13-month tour of duty as a radioman in Vietnam. Republish with permission._

## [This might be the real reason Elon Musk says Tesla’s AI5 chip is going to Optimus](https://electrek.co/2026/11/27/tesla-ai5-optimus-real-reason-tsmc-3nm/)

via Electrek Feed · 2026-11-27, updated 2026-11-28

![](https://electrek.co/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2026/04/Tesla-AI5-chip-hero.jpeg?quality=82&strip=all&w=1600)

Elon Musk says Tesla’s cars don’t need the next-generation AI5 chip, so it’s going to Optimus first. A new report from Taiwan’s chip supply chain gives a more practical reason: Tesla won’t have enough AI5 chips for its cars until 2028.

According to [Commercial Times](https://www.ctee.com.tw/news/20261127700096-439901), Tesla’s chip design partner Global Unichip (GUC) is giving its limited TSMC 3nm capacity for 2027 to Google and Microsoft data center chips first. That leaves Tesla with a relatively small AI5 allocation.

## [Under Trump, select people avoid being held responsible](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/under-trump-select-people-avoid-being-held-responsible)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-27

Big mistakes, bad acts, and ethical failures - when exposed - should lead to legal consequences. Historically that has been generally true, but we are living in a different time.

The recent [Jensen Huang affair](https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/a-timeline-of-nvidia-ceo-jensen-huangs-suspected-spy-drama-05cbebf3) exposed his professional and personal failures. As played out so far, he seems likely to not suffer consequences typically automatic for what he has admittedly done.

This outcome has become routine.

Those connected politically or financially with President Donald Trump are able to avoid being held responsible.

- The MAGA rioters who attacked the Capitol were [pardoned and released](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardon_of_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack_defendants).

- Convicted financial fraudsters [have been freed](https://www.brennancenter.org/our-work/analysis-opinion/political-donors-should-not-be-above-law) after contributing to Trump projects.

- [MAGA political sympathizers](https://www.congress.gov/119/meeting/house/118671/documents/HHRG-119-JU13-20251119-SD017.pdf) who committed crimes have been pardoned and let loose.

- The former [President of Honduras was pardoned](https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/IN12621) from a drug smuggling conviction.

- [Judgicial nominees](https://demandjustice.org/judicialreport/) repeat Trump lies with impunity and still get approved.

- [Fraudulent, phony state presidential electors from 2020](https://www.citizensforethics.org/reports-investigations/crew-reports/the-cases-against-fake-electors-and-where-they-stand/) have gone unpunished.

- President Putin has been [charged with war crimes](https://www.icc-cpi.int/defendant/vladimir-vladimirovich-putin) and maintains the status of [best buddy](https://www.ms.now/news/trump-putin-xi-russia-china-bromance).

- [Epstein files](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cn5gzepnw4lo) stay concealed and redacted.


Examples of unaccountability abound.

Our South Dakota ex-governor is a prime illustration.

Kristy Noem’s disastrous time as Secretary of Homeland Security put poorly managed, poorly trained ICE agents on the streets. While Noem repeatedly lied with her “worst of the worst” characterization of detainees, her officers hid behind their masks, [allegedly violated constitutional rights](https://www.aclu.org/cases/tincher-v-noem-et-al), and left two innocent residents dead in Minneapolis. [Noem called them “domestic terrorists](https://www.nbcnews.com/news/us-news/noem-doesnt-retract-calling-us-citizens-killed-immigration-agents-dome-rcna261499).

What was the consequence of this historically inept time as a cabinet secretary?

She was appointed to a phony-baloney new federal job and so far she and [her agents who killed people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_shootings_by_U.S._immigration_agents_in_the_second_Trump_administration) have not been held responsible for those and other detainee deaths.

She’s just another Trump person dancing away from deserved consequences.

Her day may come when [alleged no-bid sweetheart deals](https://www.welch.senate.gov/welch-calls-on-dhs-to-investigate-secretary-noems-220-million-ad-campaign/) are investigated by the new congress but for now she’s MAGA safe.

Why are we in this “no fault zone”?

Simple answer:  We have a Republican Party that has, top to bottom, failed the country in not pushing back on atrocities of the Trump time in office.  He does it because he can, and he can because party members who should object just roll over. Some are showing backbone now, but it’s too late.

They could have avoided this with an impeachment after Trump sent his MAGA Army to attack Congress, but lacked the integrity or courage to do it. We should never forgive or forget.

As a consequence, we have 25 more months where the rule of law, and blame for bad acts are not applicable to select citizens of our USA.  One can’t imagine what’s to come.

_Mike Levsen is a former mayor of Aberdeen_

_Photo: public domain,_ [_wikimedia commons_](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Attackers_assault_law_enforcement_during_January_6_United_States_Capitol_attack.png)

It's that time of year when we've all got food on the brain. Images of glistening turkeys and steaming pies right out of the oven. But, increasingly out in the world, you might have noticed those kinds of images looking a bit off. Thanks to AI.

It's the topic for today's “ [Uncanny AI](https://www.marketplace.org/uncanny-ai),” our regular series about those times AI just makes us feel icky. [Brady Brickner-Wood](https://www.bradybricknerwood.com/) wrote about the scourge of AI food images for The New Yorker. He said the worst examples can look sweaty, unnaturally fractal, almost insect-like.

## [Linda M. Hasselstrom: “Those Thanksgiving Day Pie-Makers”](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/those-thanksgiving-day-pie-makers)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-26, updated 2026-11-27

_Editor’s note: Linda M. Hasselstrom’s evocation of holiday pie-making was first published in “Empty Bowls 2006” by the United Church of Christ, Brookings, SD. In 2011 it was published by The Backwaters Press in a collection titled_ [_Dirt Songs: A Plains Duet_](https://www.amazon.com/Dirt-Songs-Twyla-M-Hansen/dp/1935218247) _, with Twyla M. Hansen. I thank Ms. Hasselstrom for her permission to post it here._

_Author’s note: When I wrote this poem, my husband Jerry Ellerman and I were living in Cheyenne, Wyoming, in a tall old house on the corner of Warren Avenue and 27th Street, hence the reference to the 'drug dealers next door.’ Now that I am back on the ranch, there are no houses next to mine._

All over America today, women search

for their grandmother’s pumpkin pie recipe.

Some rush to the store for condensed milk,

or whipping cream. Or stir up powdered milk

if they are poor, or on a diet,

or live too far from town.

In a Wisconsin farm house a red-haired woman

measures salt in a dented spoon.

In California, a thin girl stirs and puffs a cigarette,

puffs and stirs. In Wyoming,

I dust clove powder over my grandmother’s

green glass bowl and reach for the nutmeg grater.

In New Mexico, a brown-eyed woman

sprinkles cayenne. In Iowa, a man beats eggs,

recalling for his children how their mother looked.

Grandma always left me to measure

dry ingredients while she walked down

to her hen house. She came back holding four

warm brown eggs in her open hands

just as I licked brown sugar off my lips,

thinking she wouldn’t notice.

So today, twenty-five years after she died,

I lap brown sugar from a spoon just

so I’ll remember how she grinned at me.

While I stir, my oven beeps. Hers

was fired with wood she chopped. To test

the heat, she’d dip her fingers

in the water bucket she’d pumped full

that morning, flick spattering drops, and nod.

All over America, families are studying

gratitude. Some women slip

a pie into the oven, and hide

the cardboard box in the garbage.

Others light pumpkin-scented candles,

thankful anyway– though my grandmother

might not think they have good reason.

I crimp the rim of each pie crust

with three fingers, just the way

she taught me; make a salad

while the fragrance surges out

the open kitchen window. Next door,

perhaps the drug dealers open their eyes,

inhale, and almost remember.

Grandmother, may this pumpkin perfume

rise up to whatever heaven you inhabit,

sanctifying all my love and memories.

Listen: countless voices chant together

an infinity of thankful hymns.

© 2006, Linda M. Hasselstrom

_South Dakota rancher Linda M. Hasselstrom writes poetry and nonfiction. She is the_ [_winner_](https://sdexcellence.org/Linda_M._Hasselstrom) _of numerous awards._

_Photo: public domain,_ [_wikimedia commons_](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Pumpkin_Pie.jpg)

Linda M. Hasselstrom

[lindamichele777@gmail.com](mailto:lindamichele777@gmail.com)

PO Box 169 Hermosa, SD 57744

Website: [www.WindbreakHouse.com](http://www.windbreakhouse.com/)

Facebook: [www.Facebook.com/WindbreakHouse](http://www.facebook.com/WindbreakHouse)

Blog: [WindbreakHouse.WordPress.com](https://windbreakhouse.wordpress.com/)

South Dakota Living Poet of Merit, 2020

Author of:

_Beginning, Again_ (prose and poems with photographs by James W. Parker

_Walking: The Changes_ (poems with photographs by James W. Parker)

Windbreak: A Woman Rancher on the Northern Plains (2nd edition 2026, with photos)

_Write Now, Here’s How – Insights from Six Decades of Writing_ (August, 2020)

_Gathering from the Grassland: A Plains Journal_

_Dakota: Bones, Grass, Sky_, collected and new poems

_The Wheel of the Year: A Writer's Workbook_

_Dirt Songs: A Plains Duet,_ with Twyla M. Hansen; _No Place Like Home: Notes from a Western Life, Between Grass and Sky, Feels Like Far, Bitter Creek Junction, Land Circle, Dakota Bones, Going Over East, Bison: Monarch of the Plains, When a Poet Dies, The Roadside History of South Dakota, Roadkill, Caught By One Wing_.

Editor of _Leaning into the Wind, Woven on the Wind, Crazy Woman Creek_ with Gaydell Collier and Nancy Curtis; also editor of _Journal of a Mountain Man_, by James Clyman.

## [Army calls detonation of bomb chemicals in Iowa a success after earlier fears of a toxic plume](https://federalnewsnetwork.com/army/2026/11/us-stockpile-of-unstable-and-dangerous-lead-azide-scheduled-for-detonation-in-iowa/)

via Federal News Network · 2026-11-26, updated 2026-11-27

The U.S. Army has blown up the nation’s entire stockpile of lead azide, a chemical used for explosives, because it had deteriorated and might explode uncontrollably. Officials say the first checks show no toxic dust spread to nearby homes and farms. The Army called Wednesday's detonation a success, with all 5 tons destroyed. The Environmental Protection Agency reported no lead in the air around the plant. Still, government agencies will do more testing in the coming days. Lead is a heavy metal that’s especially dangerous to children and pregnant women.

## [Pentagon hires conservative contractors to audit military base schools and pilot ‘classical’ PE](https://federalnewsnetwork.com/defense-news/2026/11/pentagon-hires-conservative-contractors-to-audit-military-base-schools-and-pilot-classical-pe/)

via Federal News Network · 2026-11-25, updated 2026-11-26

The Defense Department continues to revamp its schools for military families to align with conservative education ideals. The Department of Defense Education Activity operates about 160 schools across 11 countries, educating children of service members. It has begun auditing curriculum and piloting what's called a "classical" physical education course at two U.S. campuses — in North Carolina and Virginia. The department awarded a $5.7 million contract to conservative-aligned American Education Solutions for a curriculum audit. A separate contract of around $100,000 was awarded to Iliad Athletics to start “classical” P.E., inspired by traditional Greek athletics. A Pentagon spokesperson says the agency regularly reviews its academic programs and makes improvements.

via Market Place Tech Report · 2026-11-25

## [Tom Lawrence asked: What are you thankful for? Still a good question](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/an-ode-to-thanksgiving-by-the-late-tom-lawrence)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-25, updated 2026-11-27

_Editor’s note:_ _The Standard’s co-founder, -publisher and -editor Tom Lawrence, who died last August, wrote this in 2024._

What are you thankful for this year?

Not just on Thursday, when we celebrate [Thanksgiving](https://www.timeanddate.com/holidays/us/thanksgiving-day). What are you appreciative of today?

Your family? Have you been loved and cherished and supported by your parents, your siblings, grandparents, aunts and uncles, cousins and other relatives? Have they been there for you at happy moments, like when you graduated from school, won an award, gotten married or enjoyed a party?

Were they at your side when someone you all loved died? Did they come to your assistance when you were sick, broke or in need of a ride?

Or did they just tell you they cared about you, were proud of you, loved you?

Be thankful for them.

How about your friends? Do you recall the times you laughed so hard it hurt? How about when you felt down in the dumps and they came to spend time with you, or called to check in? Remember all the days you spent together in school, at work, or out having fun?

Be thankful for them.

Who has offered you help, guidance or encouragement along the way? What neighbors, teachers, co-workers, bosses and others gave you boosts in your life and career? Who played a role in getting you to the place you are at today? No one does it alone.

Thank those who offered you a hand along the way.

How’s your health? Are you feeling pretty good right now? That’s certainly worth noting. Not on top of the world? Let’s hope you are receiving quality care from experienced professionals who are dedicated to helping you recover or at least survive.

Be thankful for that.

Got a job? A warm, safe home? Food in the fridge and at least a few dollars in your wallet? Millions of people across the planet don’t have any of those.

So, be thankful for all of those things, too.

What members of your community are contributing to your life? What public servants, business owners and employees and local residents have made this beautiful, peaceful region such a marvelous place to live?

They deserve thanks, too.

Are you appreciating the glories of this world? Every day, a different masterpiece is painted on our skies. The grass, trees, fields and plants offer a paintbox filled with beauty. Animals, from our pets to livestock who mean so much to our economy and lifestyles, to the birds that provide music in our world and the wild creatures who offer mystery and excitement, are among the greatest gifts we have ever received.

Not every moment is pleasant. There are tough days and rough patches. They are all part of the road we walk.

Give thanks to the lessons you learned as you made it through those difficult times. They have a value, too.

On holidays, we look forward to happy times with the people we love. Look back on those memories now. Hold them close.

And be thankful you have them in your memory and heart.

Lastly, be thankful for this second, this minute, this hour and this day. You are here, and that means so much. There are many opportunities ahead, no matter your age or condition. You might not run as fast or work as hard as you could, and you might not be able to do all you once did, but if you look around and consider the options presented to you, it’s apparent there is much to be thankful for right now.

So, give thanks. Enjoy the moment and the day, and be grateful for the people in your life, because Thanksgiving deserves more respect.

All too often, it’s the holiday between Halloween, which has turned into a two-month celebration of orange and black, candy and treats, ghost stories and parties, and the [deluge of Christmas](https://www.lapost.com/christmas-creep-accelerates-as-holiday-season-starts-earlier-each-year?sid=lapaw_search_dynamic_lp2&gad_source=1&gbraid=0AAAAAqWQzu2hhrKLjws_DvR8RNBV12SxB&gclid=CjwKCAiA3ZC6Bh), which now lasts for most of the fall and early winter. Did it start in August this year?

Meanwhile, Thanksgiving is over and out in a day. Cook the turkey, sit down with the family for an hour or less, watch some football and it’s on to Black Friday! Let’s do some Christmas shopping!

But that doesn’t have to be the case. We can honor the history and true significance of this autumnal gathering in a respectful manner. We can actually dedicate some time to giving thanks to the people who we love and cherish, and to the things that really matter.

Thanksgiving is the most peaceful holiday of the year.

No gift-shopping required. No costumes. No late-night fractious parties. Thanksgiving is all about enjoying an easy, relaxing day. Family. Friends. Food. Football.

So, don’t rush through Thursday. Spend as much time as possible with your family and friends, and really talk with each other. Ask them what they are grateful for this year.

Share old stories that elicit smiles and laughter. Talk about something other than politics or business or sports. Remember the real reason we are coming together on this date.

Thanksgiving deserves that — and so do you.

_Fourth-generation South Dakotan Tom Lawrence has written for several newspapers and websites in South Dakota and other states for four decades. He has contributed to The New York Times, NPR, The London Telegraph, The Daily Beast and other media outlets. Do not republish without permission._

_Photo: public domain,_ [_wikimedia commons_](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:President_Trump_Pardons_the_National_Thanksgiving_Turkey_(50679370291).jpg)

If you'd like to help us continue our mission to advance independent political and social commentary, you can do so by clicking on the "Donate" button that's on the sidebar to your right._**

## [Army to detonate US stockpile of bomb-making chemicals in Iowa the day before Thanksgiving](https://federalnewsnetwork.com/army/2026/11/army-says-weather-conditions-favorable-for-wednesday-detonation-of-bomb-making-material-in-iowa/)

via Federal News Network · 2026-11-25, updated 2026-11-26

The U.S. Army says it plans to detonate the nation's entire stockpile of lead azide in Iowa on Wednesday morning. It says waiting any longer raises the risks that the dangerously degrading bomb-making chemicals could explode at any time. Officials sought to assure residents that they’ve taken steps to put barriers around the blast zone and limit the plume from the detonation when it happens the day before Thanksgiving. Acting Secretary of the Army Adam Telle said Tuesday there's no reason to panic. The controlled detonation at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant in Middletown has set off concerns about potential health and environmental effects and frustration over a lack of information.

## [Two tech companies tried to reinvent the e-bike. Why DJI’s Amflow soared while Rivian’s ALSO floundered](https://electrek.co/2026/11/24/two-tech-companies-tried-to-reinvent-the-e-bike-why-djis-amflow-soared-while-rivians-also-floundered/)

via Electrek Feed · 2026-11-24, updated 2026-11-28

![](https://electrek.co/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2026/11/amflow-vs-also-head.jpg?quality=82&strip=all&w=1600)

Two of the most interesting new electric bicycle companies in the world made headlines on the same day last week, and for two very different reasons.

## [Money and food, the two things in life that we cannot quit](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/money-and-food-the-two-things-we-cannot-quit)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-24, updated 2026-11-27

Someone recovering from an addiction to alcohol or drugs can stop drinking or using. But none of us get to stop eating or spending. Whatever difficult relationships we may have with food or money, we need to recover and make behavioral changes while still using them every day.

More than 20 years ago, a colleague and I developed one of the earliest financial therapy workshops. At one meal, we asked participants to practice conscious eating. First, we smelled our food for a full minute. For the next ten minutes, we ate in silence, setting our forks down between each bite. Whenever I did this, I usually felt full and finished with half the food still on my plate. Eating this slowly shows how much we consume without noticing.

My rough estimate is that about 20 percent of problems with either money or food are cognitive: you do not know something, someone tells you, and you fix it. For example, at 35, my waistline started growing even though my eating habits had not changed. Then I learned about metabolism. I stopped pouring cream on my cereal and started losing weight.

The other 80-some percent of money or food problems are not changed by information, because they are not about the money or the food.

The two problems often show up together. A 2009 study by Eva Münster and colleagues at the University of Mainz, titled “ [Over-indebtedness as a marker of socioeconomic status and its association with obesity](https://bmcpublichealth.biomedcentral.com/articles/10.1186/1471-2458-9-286),” surveyed 949 people in debt counseling in two German states. It found that 25.1 percent were obese, compared with 11.3 percent of the general population. This was after accounting for age, sex, education, income, depression, and smoking.

That study looked at people at one point in time, so it could not say which problem came first. Economists Matthias Keese and Hendrik Schmitz later [followed German households from 1999 to 2009](https://doi.org/10.1111/roiw.12002) and found that debt went on to worsen physical and mental health, but not weight. I suspect that more often, rather than one problem causing the other, the two share a deeper root.

A recent guest on my podcast has built her practice on that overlap. [Jaelyn Vickery](https://www.jaevic.com/), a Certified Financial Therapist with a master’s degree in social work, specializes in serving clients who struggle with both food and money.

She describes what she calls disordered consumption, a cycle with three parts. Compulsion is the urge, often driven by unrecognized stress, loneliness, or boredom. Consumption is the behavior, such as overspending or overeating. Control is the response that follows: guilt, shame, secrecy, and attempts to regain control through restriction or self-criticism. In her view, the control phase keeps the cycle running, because it never meets the need that started the urge.

Ted Klontz, who helped me build those first workshops, used to say that whatever the compulsion, the path leads to the same living room, which is the trauma underneath. One of our workshops had  eight participants, all there for help with money issues. By the end of the week, six of them had traced their money behavior back to sexual abuse.

If either food or money has been a struggle for you, I suggest starting with the compulsion/urge part of the pattern rather than trying yet another diet or budget. This can begin with a simple strategy. When you find yourself reaching for an unneeded snack or opening a shopping app, pause. Wait ten minutes and write down what you are feeling. This can give you an entry point for exploring what lies beneath the urge. You also may find some useful insights at Vickery’s [Finance, Fitness & Finesse](https://www.financefitnessfinesse.com/) podcast.

_Rick Kahler, CFP®, CFT™, is a pioneer in financial therapy and a 2026 inductee into the Financial Therapy Hall of Fame. He is the founder of The Financial Therapy Podcast and Kahler Financial Group, and co-author of “Coupleship Inc.” and “The Financial Wisdom of Ebenezer Scrooge._

## [Democratic Rep. Jimmy Gomez resigns after Ethics panel finds evidence of sexual misconduct](https://federalnewsnetwork.com/congress/2026/11/ethics-probe-finds-democratic-rep-jimmy-gomez-made-unwelcome-sexual-advances-to-a-staffer/)

via Federal News Network · 2026-11-23, updated 2026-11-25

Democratic Rep. Jimmy Gomez of California is resigning from Congress after the House Ethics Committee released a report finding “substantial evidence” he engaged in sexual misconduct toward a staffer. The committee said in a report Monday that Gomez's unwelcome sexual advances and sexual contact caused the aide significant and ongoing distress and contributed to her decision to leave House employment. Gomez said he has “strong disagreements" with the Ethics panel's findings but announced he will resign from Congress on Dec. 1. Gomez was already set to depart Congress at the end of the year, having been defeated in his bid for reelection this month by a fellow Democrat.

## [Sioux Falls city council rezoning for data center is premature](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/after-sioux-falls-rezoning-raises-questions-data-center-approvals-should-be-paused)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-23, updated 2026-11-27

Upon further review:

Earlier this year the Sioux Falls City Council approved rezoning land in the northeast part of the city. The rezoning was to allow the construction of a large-scale data center. In this part of town is the city’s wastewater treatment plant, an Xcel Energy power station, and other non-residential uses. There are residences about a mile away, including many in Brandon.

The night the rezoning was approved, the Council chamber was filled with citizens expressing concerns and asking questions about the long-term impacts. The rezoning passed on a 7-0 vote. Folks were not happy.

Since that night, hundreds of stories have been published around the country, discussing the issues with hyper-scale data centers.

Environmental concerns impacting people living and working nearby. Noise. Air quality. Water usage. Power demands.

On November 3rd, numerous ballot issues around the country were passed either restricting or banning super-sized data centers.

The City Council recently revived a four-member working group, the Regulatory Oversight Committee, made up of Council members to learn more and provide intelligent and rational ordinances to deal with the issues.

Meanwhile, numerous citizens have taken it upon themselves to learn more and be visible with their concerns.

There is talk of a citizen petition drive allowing the voters to be heard on this topic. A one-year moratorium is part the discussion.

Data centers exist here. Outside of Garretson is the EROS Data Center. It has been there since the early 1970s. SDN Communications has one. Neither are super-sized. There are other small ones here and around the state. None of the existing data centers fall into the category of super-sized.

To be fair, our usage of cell phones, IPads, laptops, and other digital devices is driving part the demand for large-scale data centers.

Three years ago, most of us had not heard of them. There was very little information available on the environmental impacts. Now, there appears to be plenty.

Governments at the Federal, State, and Local level did not get ahead on this issue. No in-depth studies. No rules or regulations.

The feds, by order of President Trump, are NOT going to act. Our state legislature passed a “Data Center Bill of Rights” last session. According to folks reading it, it’s a nice start, but doesn’t do much to deal with the problems. Nor does it provide consequences for violations of the rules.

Consequently, cities and counties are the only units of government directly involved. Regulating and monitoring data centers requires highly technical people. Not many of those in our state, including Sioux Falls.

Data Centers are not currently classified as a public utility, however they should be regulated like one, so issues like location, noise, power demands, water usage, and air-quality can be appropriately decided.

If the City Council doesn’t adequately respond quickly, I suspect a citizen petition drive will proceed.

For the record I am not anti-business, nor anti-innovation. I am pro-public health and safety.

Upon further review, a pause seems prudent. That pause should include the current proposal.

_Rick Knobe is a former mayor of Sioux Falls and long-time radio talk show host, now retired._

_Graphic: public domain,_ [_wikimedia commons_](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Dissipazione_termica_e_innalzamento_delle_temperature_locali_causati_da_un_Data_Center.webp)

## [Rounds, Thune split on bill that speeds up project permitting process](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/rounds-thune-split-on-permitting-reform-bill-that-trump-supports)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-21, updated 2026-11-27

Our newly re-elected U.S. Sen. Mike Rounds just showed some willingness to buck his party by voting against a major permitting reform bill. It’s called the [Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026](https://www.energy.senate.gov/press/dem/heinrich-lee-capito-and-whitehouse-introduce-bipartisan-american-affordability-and-jobs-act-of-2026/) (BAAJA) and it's about 500 pages long. You can read a 2-page summation [here](https://www.energy.senate.gov/press/dem/heinrich-lee-capito-and-whitehouse-introduce-bipartisan-american-affordability-and-jobs-act-of-2026/).

Basically, BAAJA streamlines the permitting process for energy projects. [Time savings](https://www.energypolicy.columbia.edu/how-the-senate-permitting-reform-bill-could-impact-energy-project-timelines/) are significant. Depending on the project, years of permitting could be reduced to just months if BAAJA passes.

Anybody familiar with governmental red tape understands the appeal of a law like this. In fact, the bill just passed the senate 62-33. Sen. Thune voted aye.

Rounds explained his nay by [claiming](https://www.sdpb.org/politics/2026-11-20/rounds-votes-no-thune-votes-yes-as-senate-passes-permitting-reform-bill) that the bill infringes on state and local rights and gives developers a built-in advantage by transferring contested cases to federal regulators if they haven’t been settled locally after 12 months.

Thune [supports the bill](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dDy2Rw3D3l8) because it eliminates bureaucratic delays, spurs the economy and creates jobs.

I haven’t seen environmental issues being addressed by either senator.

Reflexively – and as it turned out, incorrectly – I thought environmental interests would be uniformly opposed to legislation that speeded up energy-related projects. Turns out, there’s some ambivalence among environmentalists when it comes to this bill. An AI overview concludes that “environmental groups are divided over the [Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act](https://www.energy.senate.gov/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/FACT-SHEET-Bipartisan-American-Affordability-and-Jobs-Act3.pdf) (BAAJA), with many major organizations opposing the bill while some clean energy and climate advocates support it.”

As to opponents:

- The [National Resources Defense Council](https://www.nrdc.org/media/nrdc-assessment-baaja-2026-permitting-transmission-bill) says the bill weakens environmental safeguards and public participation.

- [The Climate Justice Alliance](https://climatejusticealliance.org/100ej-groups-letter-opposing-baaja/) says it expands fossil fuel infrastructure and harms frontline communities


As to supportive environmental groups:

- The [Citizens Climate Lobby](https://yaleclimateconnections.org/2026/11/baaja-whos-in-favor-and-whos-opposed/) says permitting bottlenecks slow down environmentally friendly projects.

- The [Solar Energy and Industries Association](https://seia.org/media/18668/baaja-industry-letter-11-9-2026.pdf/) says it will put downward pressure on energy prices, and deploy more affordable, reliable solar and storage for American families and businesses.


As to me, what do I know? Only this:

Those generalized arguments, pro and con, are plenty relevant, of course, but nowadays the compelling question is how this legislation will affect the development of data centers. Much as I appreciate Thune’s economic development approach, I lean toward Rounds’s local control argument. I don’t like that after a year, disputes over energy development, including data centers, will automatically move from local to federal regulation if BAAJA passes the House of Representatives.

The prospect of a vote in the current House [seems iffy](https://heatmap.news/politics/baaja-house), but we’ll find out soon enough. My hope is that it will stall and get an intra-party test in the next Democratically-weighted congress, which is a fresh representation of how the voting public leans on a matter as significant as this one.

_John Tsitrian is a businessman and writer from the Black Hills. He was a weekly columnist for the Rapid City Journal for 20 years. His articles and commentary have also appeared in The Los Angeles Times, The Denver Post and The Omaha World-Herald. Tsitrian served in the Marines for three years (1966-69), including a 13-month tour of duty as a radioman in Vietnam. Republish with permission._

## [Just how much have energy costs raised core inflation?](https://www.marketplace.org/episode/2026/11/20/just-how-much-have-energy-costs-raised-core-inflation)

via Market Place · 2026-11-20

Economists use “core” inflation to describe price changes minus food and energy (which can be up one week and down the next). But for the past six months, energy prices have been stubbornly high, as the White House drags out its war in Iran. That sustained inflation is having a trickle-down impact. In this episode, why core inflation can’t escape fuel costs. Plus: Retailers cater to picky consumers, and the stock market tumbles after a historic IPO.

## [Trump’s “friend” Xi is lagging on his promise to buy U.S. grain](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/trumps-chinese-friend-xi-is-lagging-on-his-commitment-to-buy-american-farm-goods)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-20, updated 2026-11-27

Given the way President Trump was [fawning](https://www.thedailybeast.com/suck-up-trump-fawns-over-friend-at-dictator-lovefest) all over Chinese President Xi Jinping during their meeting last week in Shenzen, China, you’d think the two pals were having a friendly, upfront and cordial get-together. After the meeting, Trump even went so far as to describe their relationship as [“very special,”](https://www.profarmer.com/news/first-thing-today/first-thing-today-trump-asks-xi-do-some-more-our-farmers) which might have warmed a lot of people’s hearts … unless you’re among the people who are depending on China to live up to its [agreements](https://www.farmprogress.com/farm-policy/china-s-17-billion-crop-pledge-falters-ahead-of-trump-xi-summit) on purchasing American farm products.

If you are, then you know that American producers are [still waiting](https://www.farmprogress.com/farm-policy/china-s-17-billion-crop-pledge-falters-ahead-of-trump-xi-summit) on $17 billion worth of Chinese grain orders [that were promised last spring](https://www.whitehouse.gov/fact-sheets/2026/05/fact-sheet-president-donald-j-trump-secures-historic-deals-with-china-delivering-for-american-workers-farmers-and-industry/), when Trump and Xi had a similar meeting.

Yes, China has been doing some on-schedule buying of soybeans, but it looks like the purchase commitment for corn and wheat that was supposed to be completed in 2026 probably [won’t happen](https://farmpolicynews.illinois.edu/2026/11/china-unlikely-to-fulfill-17-billion-us-ag-purchase-pledge-this-year/). According to the University of Illinois’ Farm Policy News, analysts at Bloomberg say they “would not be counting on China to buy corn or wheat from the US in the next 30 days.”

During the course of the meeting, Trump asked Xi [“to do more for our farmers,”](https://www.yahoo.com/news/politics/articles/trump-explodes-weird-caps-rant-140352297.html) which is a curiously passive reaction to the fact that China seems to be reneging on its commitments — and certainly doesn’t sound like the president is fighting for American producers.

Naturally, the chronically self-deluded Donald J. Trump doesn’t see it that way. On the flight back to the States he insisted on [social media](https://www.forth.news/lists/trumpts/ChCP4tqxCCfbaAmMQUtUX) that “there has been no greater president that stands with American, farmers and ranchers than President Donald J. Trump,” an assertion that’s kind of laughable considering that [rural districts, on the whole, shifted sharply](https://www.facebook.com/NEWSMAX/posts/rural-voters-across-the-country-shifted-sharply-toward-democrats-in-tuesdays-mid/1730930445746217/) toward Democrats during last week’s midterms. The [majority of rural voters disapprove](https://www.ms.now/opinion/rural-voters-trump-poll) of Trump, whose claim that he has been a great friend to farmers and ranchers was buried under an avalanche of rejection on election day.

If Trump’s relationship with Xi is so “special,” he should be able to look him in the eye and tell the Chinese leader that  he needs to live up to his purchase agreements and buy American grain as he promised he would.

Meantime, 2027 looks like [yet another tough year](https://www.farmaid.org/issues/challenges-family-farmers-face/tracking-the-farm-economy-in-crisis/) for the ag industry, with the [American Farm Bureau](https://www.fb.org/intel/markets/crop-costs-revised-higher-with-little-relief-in-sight) predicting that production costs will reach record highs and crop prices will remain low.

It’s nice that Trump thinks he “stands with American” producers, but the problem is that that’s all he’s doing. He’s just standing there.

Rural voters, who saw through him in a very decisive way, have made it clear that they’ve had enough.

_John Tsitrian is a businessman and writer from the Black Hills. He was a weekly columnist for the Rapid City Journal for 20 years. His articles and commentary have also appeared in The Los Angeles Times, The Denver Post and The Omaha World-Herald. Tsitrian served in the Marines for three years (1966-69), including a 13-month tour of duty as a radioman in Vietnam. Republish with permission._

## [The disconnect among South Dakota voters confuses me](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/south-dakota-voters-confuse-me)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-19, updated 2026-11-27

Over the past several years South Dakota voters have created public policy that lifts people up and reinforces the state motto, “Under God, the people rule.”

Examples: Raising the state minimum wage; banning predatory lenders; approving medical marijuana. This past election we protected the expansion of Medicaid, and saved majority rule for changing the state constitution.

All these public votes lifted people up and protected the will of the people.

Here is my confusion:

Why do we continue to elect people who want to control our lives taking away the freedom our state leaders always brag about?

Why do we continue to elect folks who have pledged their loyalty to the man who is trying to destroy our country, our way of life?

Maybe it’s this?

Over the past 40 years, Republicans have continually told us Democrats are baby killers and want to take away our guns. This effort is at the federal and state level.

The brainwashing has apparently worked. All Republicans are good. All Democrats are bad, even evil.

For the record, I have never met anyone who wants to kill babies. I have not met a Democrat who wants to take away guns. Yet that lie persists.

Several years ago, there was a citizen led effort to create Open Primaries in South Dakota.

Since all of us pay for elections, it made sense to allow all of us to vote. One of the provisions in the effort was the removal of party labels on the ballot. No R’s, D’s, I’s, or other labels. The candidate’s name only.

The proposal failed.

A man I know and used to respect told me he was voting against it because he needed to know what party a candidate belonged to. He was a R. He only voted for Rs. No investigating the candidate’s positions or philosophy. No deep thinking. Rs are good. Ds are not.

To me, he represents the problem. Rs win here because they have convinced themselves and too many of us that Rs are good, Ds are not, and Independents are spineless, with no core beliefs.

This election cycle at the national level Republicans lost just about everywhere. The Senate and the House have flipped. Numerous non-Rs are moving into Governor’s mansions.

Numerous non-Rs won Secretary of State, Attorney General and other statewide races. State Legislatures flipped as well.

But not here. We sent back to the U.S. Senate, a man who has voted almost 99% of the time supporting Trump. Those votes hurt us. We have elected a congressman who had his picture taken with Trump, both-of-them smiling and giving the thumbs up sign. We elected a Governor who has bent his knee to Trump.

Why do we do that?

Our state legislature will continue with Rs in the super-majority, despite our knowledge that divided government is good government. It brings out new ideas and new approaches.

So it’s clear, We, the People know good public policy when we see it. We approve it. But we elect people loyal to a man, not the constitution, not the country, not us.

Why?

A line from the popular folk song “Where have all the flowers gone?” comes to mind.

“When will we ever learn? When will WE ever learn?”

_Rick Knobe is a former mayor of Sioux Falls and long-time radio talk show host, now retired._

## [The trouble with one-and-done financial solutions like loan consolidations](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/the-trouble-with-one-and-done-financial-solutions)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-18, updated 2026-11-27

Sometime the solution to a money problem seems to be a “one and done” fix. Maybe you’re having trouble paying bills and are accumulating debt. Then you receive an unexpected tax refund, get a raise, or refinance your mortgage, so everything should be better. Each of those can help, but none of them by itself changes the underlying patterns that caused the problem.

A debt consolidation loan is a clear example. It may provide lower rates and payments that offer a respite. Yet the relief may not last.  A 2023 study by TransUnion, titled “ [Debt Consolidation in a Rising Economy](https://newsroom.transunion.com/debt-consolidation-aug2023/),” found that people who used a personal loan to pay off credit cards cut their card balances by 57 percent on average. For many of them, the balances were nearly back to their earlier levels 18 months later. The loan could not change the circumstances and the spending that built the debt in the first place.

In my podcast interview with [Olena Keshysheva, a Certified Financial Planner in Canada](https://saplingwealth.ca/) who practices financial therapy, she drew a line between two forms of change that people mix up. One she calls healing, which is what happens when someone breaks an arm, comes down with an illness, or even suffers a great loss like a natural disaster or a death in the family. Whether the incident is large or small, there is something specific to recover from. The other she calls evolution, which is a gradual process that happens over a period of time.

Changing most money habits requires the second kind of recovery. A pattern that took years to build rarely changes in a single event, even one that does a great deal to solve an immediate problem. Olena says, “The transformation happens over a lifetime.”

Working with a financial therapist more commonly involves the longer process of evolution rather than healing. [Several years ago I wrote about two examples](https://advanced-wellbeing.com/insights/is-faster-financial-therapy-in-our-future/). One person was able to resolve his chronic money anxiety in five financial therapy sessions. A couple had worked with a financial therapist for more than ten years and, while they had made progress, were still overspending. Unfortunately, the longer the healing takes, the deeper the financial hole can get.

One strategy that tends to work is committing to a change small enough that you can stay present while you’re making it. Many of us, when we identify a money problem and try to fix it, choose a solution that matches how bad we feel about the problem. Often, this level of solution is big enough to overwhelm us. Then the part of us that handles being overwhelmed takes over and does what it knows how to do—which is return to the old behavior.

The kind of solution we are more able to maintain is one with less emotional weight, one that fits an ordinary week. Here’s what that may mean in practice. Instead of vowing to save your entire bonus, set up an automatic transfer to move $10 or $20 a week into saving. Instead of promising to review all your financial information every month, commit to deliberately opening one statement. A smaller, slower change that you can maintain is likely to serve you better than a larger one you abandon after nine days.

At times, of course, a large change can still be the right call. A consolidation loan, a new job, or a move may be part of the solution. It’s important, however, to see such an action as not as a fix to the problem, but as the beginning of the work. It is just one step toward changing your money behavior.

_Rick Kahler, CFP®, CFT™, is a pioneer in financial therapy and a 2026 inductee into the Financial Therapy Hall of Fame. He is the founder of The Financial Therapy Podcast and Kahler Financial Group, and co-author of “Coupleship Inc.” and “The Financial Wisdom of Ebenezer Scrooge._

## [Tesla unveils 7.4 MW Powercore to take on ex-exec Baglino’s Heron Link](https://electrek.co/2026/11/17/tesla-powercore-solid-state-transformer-heron-link/)

via Electrek Feed · 2026-11-17, updated 2026-11-28

![](https://electrek.co/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2026/11/Tesla-Powercore.jpeg?quality=82&strip=all&w=1600)

Tesla has unveiled Powercore, a solid-state medium-voltage transformer that also handles the inverter and breaker functions in a single unit. Tesla rates it at 7.4 MW (9 MVA) continuously at 34.5 kV, with grid-forming support up to 10 MVA of peak power.

It’s made in America and scheduled to start operating in 2027. That’s the same year Heron Power, the startup founded by Tesla’s former top engineer Drew Baglino, plans to start volume production of its own solid-state transformer, the Heron Link.

## [Farmers look to new Congress to fix damage caused by Trump](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/new-congress-has-to-repair-damage-caused-by-trumps-indifference-to-farmers)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-17, updated 2026-11-27

Does Trump actually care about farmers? They've been [financially stressed](https://www.theguardian.com/commentisfree/2026/oct/26/donald-trump-policy-disaster-rural-america) ever since Donald Trump took office nearly two years ago. Though farmers have had a [terrible time](https://www.fb.org/intel/markets/persistent-losses-leave-farmers-needing-economic-support) financially, the president seems indifferent. He doesn’t understand the nature of the crises his policies have caused. He sends farmers [bail-out money](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_administration_farmer_bailouts) then figures that everything is peachy-keen.

Even his rhetoric reveals that he doesn’t get it: Consistent with Trump’s long-standing  [detachment from reality](https://www.npr.org/2022/06/13/1104634760/barr-trump-detached-from-reality-jan-6), our self-deluded president actually [told farmers last spring](https://www.foxnews.com/politics/trump-rolls-out-plan-back-farmers-amid-rising-costs-pledging-golden-age) that “the golden age of American agriculture is right here and right now."

How much more out of touch can a person be?

Not much … and thanks to last week’s election, the times may be changing: Trump’s delusions of adequacy notwithstanding, the president will soon have to come to terms with reality as a new congress, [strongly represented by farm state Democrats](https://farmpolicynews.illinois.edu/2026/11/democrats-surge-to-power-in-congress-with-wins-in-farm-country/), will likely [re-shape agricultural legislation](https://civileats.com/2026/11/04/democratic-midterm-sweep-points-to-major-shifts-in-food-and-farm-policy/) during the foreseeable future.

Items on the agenda include:

_A long-delayed_ _and -overdue_ [_Farm Bill_](https://nationalaglawcenter.org/farm-bill-2026-overview/), which will be a priority if it doesn’t materialize in the current congress. Right now it’s gridlocked. Legislators seem mainly hung up on the [shift in some SNAP](https://www.thenewlede.org/2026/09/farm-bill-advances-without-controversial-save-our-bacon-act/) (formerly food stamps) benefit costs and [year-round sales of ethanol blended gasoline](https://growthenergy.org/2026/09/10/ensure-year-round-sales-of-e15/), but there are [other elements of disagreement](https://www.thenewlede.org/2026/11/midterms-farm-bill-senate/#:~:text=Democrats%20have%20made%20it%20clear,implementing%20the%20SNAP%20cost%2Dsharing%20requirement.) that need to be resolved before the new congress is sworn in.

_Doing something about input prices,_ specifically the costs of diesel fuel and fertilizer, which newly installed Democrats must find ways to lower. The only practical way of bringing those prices down is by reversing the policies that made them shoot up in the first place. Much of that has to do with the war in Iran, which needs to be concluded.

The problem with that for Dems? [Congress can’t end it unilaterally](https://www.reuters.com/world/us/can-new-us-congress-mean-an-end-iran-war-2026-11-12/). If Trump is committed to continuing the conflict, [he may find ways](https://www.nytimes.com/video/us/politics/100000011061667/does-congress-have-the-power-to-stop-the-iran-war.html) to engage in war without congressional approval. It will probably take politically vulnerable congressional Republicans, fearful of losing their jobs in ‘28, to convince Trump that the war is a continuing disaster for their party, then appeal to the president’s partisan loyalty as a way of getting this fiasco finalized.

_Finding a way to remove Trump’s_ [_stupid and disappointingly illogical tariffs_](https://www.cnbc.com/2025/04/07/trump-recession-inflation-job-losses-coming-ceos-say-cnbc-survey.html) _,_ _which have_ [_hurt farmers_](https://www.politico.com/news/2026/03/06/trump-trade-war-farmers-warning-signs-00804804). Trump has blithely set aside [generations of Republican anathema toward tariffs](https://www.npr.org/2024/12/19/nx-s1-5215953/how-the-gop-went-from-promoting-free-trade-to-backing-trumps-proposed-tariffs), and the end results have shown that his GOP predecessors were right. Tariffs stink. Democrats have [already signalled](https://www.cnbc.com/2026/11/08/trump-tariffs-democrats-congress-canada.html) that they’ll try to end Trump’s tariffs as a way of dealing with the cost-of-living, an endeavor whose promise is a big political winner, as [demonstrated](https://www.ntu.org/publications/detail/high-tariffs-impacted-the-election-democrats-gained-senate-seats-in-states-with-highest-tariff-costs-on-households) by last week’s midterms.

These and a few other [high priority items](https://www.npr.org/2026/11/05/nx-s1-6029331/democrats-have-a-long-to-do-list-when-they-take-over-congress-next-year) will dominate congressional activity next year. Trump’s failed efforts will be repudiated. In particular, his vision of a “golden age” of American agriculture will have a much better chance of materializing when farmers can see their input prices go down and their markets return to normal, unburdened by the international [grain trade disruption](https://www.pbs.org/newshour/nation/already-under-financial-pressure-farmers-squeezed-further-by-tariffs-and-iran-war) that his tariffs have created.

This will be a great opportunity for Democrats to consolidate their gains by getting it together and getting it done.

_John Tsitrian is a businessman and writer from the Black Hills. He was a weekly columnist for the Rapid City Journal for 20 years. His articles and commentary have also appeared in The Los Angeles Times, The Denver Post and The Omaha World-Herald. Tsitrian served in the Marines for three years (1966-69), including a 13-month tour of duty as a radioman in Vietnam. Republish with permission._

## [Motorcycles are notoriously dangerous. Could electric motorcycles actually change that?](https://electrek.co/2026/11/16/motorcycles-are-notoriously-dangerous-could-electric-motorcycles-actually-change-that/)

via Electrek Feed · 2026-11-16, updated 2026-11-28

![](https://electrek.co/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2024/01/ryvid-anthem-motorcycle-header.jpg?quality=82&strip=all&w=1600)

There’s no particularly comfortable way to talk about motorcycle safety. Motorcycles are dangerous. All the statistics point to the fact that per mile, motorcycles are significantly more dangerous. And as someone who promotes them as a better alternative to large cars and trucks for many trips, it’s an uncomfortable reality that must be grappled with.

But that simple statement also hides something important. Not all motorcycles, riders, or riding behaviors carry the same risk. And as a new generation of smaller electric motorcycles begins to emerge, I think there’s an interesting question worth asking: Could electric motorcycles inadvertently make motorcycling safer?

 [more…](https://electrek.co/2026/11/16/motorcycles-are-notoriously-dangerous-could-electric-motorcycles-actually-change-that/#more-491472)

## [Tesla Model X on Autopilot/FSD hit a pole and flipped, killing passenger](https://electrek.co/2026/11/16/tesla-autopilot-fsd-model-x-pole-rollover-naples/)

via Electrek Feed · 2026-11-16, updated 2026-11-28

![](https://electrek.co/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2026/11/Tesla-Model-X-autopilotfsd-crash-naples.jpeg?quality=82&strip=all&w=1600)

A Tesla Model X veered off Golden Gate Parkway in Naples, Florida, hit a traffic signal pole and overturned in September, killing a 50-year-old woman riding as a passenger. Tesla’s own report to federal regulators says Autopilot or “Full Self-Driving” was engaged.

Tesla flagged this crash itself, through its telematics, and says it has the car’s event data recorder and video. The public was told a car “veered off the road.” It’s the latest case in our [ongoing investigation into fatal crashes hidden in Tesla’s NHTSA data](https://electrek.co/2026/09/01/tesla-autopilot-fsd-crashes-hidden-data/).

## [Tesla on Autopilot/FSD was going 74 mph before fiery crash that killed 3](https://electrek.co/2026/11/16/tesla-autopilot-fsd-fiery-crash-el-dorado-hills/)

via Electrek Feed · 2026-11-16, updated 2026-11-28

![](https://electrek.co/wp-content/uploads/sites/3/2021/08/Tesla-Full-Self-Driving-Beta-Hero.jpg?quality=82&strip=all&w=1600)

A Tesla Model Y left a parkway in El Dorado Hills, California, hit a tree and burst into flames in September, killing three of the four people inside. Tesla’s own report to federal regulators says Autopilot or “Full Self-Driving” was engaged, with the car doing 74 mph.

The crash was widely covered in the Sacramento area as a fiery single-car wreck. None of that coverage mentioned a driver-assist system. It’s the latest case in our [ongoing investigation into fatal crashes hidden in Tesla’s NHTSA data](https://electrek.co/2026/09/01/tesla-autopilot-fsd-crashes-hidden-data/).

## [Roman emperor Julius Caesar used AI to write The Gallic Wars](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/roman-emperor-julius-caesar-used-ai-to-write-the-gallic-wars)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-16, updated 2026-11-27

I previously wrote an [opinion piece](https://www.tsln.com/news/ganje-beware-the-manipulators-of-truth-by-those-in-power/) on the risks of reliance on AI.

The legal profession and judiciary by way of example are not sure what to do about AI.  About nine months ago while the United States District Court was considering rules for the use of AI, I submitted a proposed rule. The court has yet to consider the issue.

The ABA in a recent article stated,  “Judiciary efforts to create rules for artificial intelligence use in the courts have been stalled amid concerns over the feasibility of reaching consensus on how the issues should be handled. The Advisory Committee on Evidence Rules, which advises the Judicial Conference of the United States, has been developing rules for AI use for several years, but efforts have encountered setbacks and resistance, slowing down the possibility of any new policy.”

AI is not the bogeyman.  It is man at his best and at his worst.  Jaron Lanier is a computer scientist and virtual reality pioneer working for Microsoft.  Lanier argues that "artificial intelligence" does not exist as an independent, thinking entity. He describes AI as a form of large-scale social collaboration.

Stanford computer science Professor Fei-Fei Li stated in _The Daily Princetonian,_“I always say there’s nothing artificial about artificial intelligence. It’s made by humans, it’s deployed by humans, it’s used by humans, and it’s governed by humans.”  Please consider these ‘human-related-comments’ as we explore the form of AI employed by Julius Caesar.

Julius Caesar’s [_The Gallic Wars_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallic_Wars) is described as his first-hand account of the several year military conflicts in which Caesar ‘on behalf of Rome’ was imposing Rome’s will on its territorial holdings in what is now greater France and parts of Germany.

History tells us that Caesar himself wrote the book except for the very last part which was written after his death by Caesar’s confidante Aulus Hirtius. The book is a well written, universally recognized classic and is a self-laudatory memoir.  Memoirs, particularly military ones, are most often self-laudatory.  Caesar wanted to shape public opinion in Rome and burnish his military achievements.

Caesar is not the true author of the book.  He presented the book as his writing.  Caesar used AI to write the book. This revelation, only recently introduced to me, bothered me tremendously.  I previously read two modern biographies of Caesar, and neither mentioned the issue.  One of the biographies devoted a chapter to analyzing the book.  I then came upon an article written a few years ago by Latin scholar Ruth Breindel on the authorship question of _The Gallic Wars_.

Ms. Breindel has a supportable opinion on who wrote _The Gallic Wars._ “After an exhaustive search of style in Book 1 of the _Gallic Wars_, focusing on the purpose clause using the subjunctive versus gerunds or gerundives, it is obvious that there were at least two or three scribes at work. . . . . So, to answer the question: who wrote the Gallic Wars?  I suggest it was a collaboration between the scribes and Caesar, and not Caesar alone.”

Breindel also wrote,  “. . . the one who is deciding on the Latin style (with Caesar’s approval, of course), is the scribe.”  Ruth Breindel from the _New England Classical Journal_: Vol. 48 : Iss. 2 , 1-11.

Whether a writing is done by an outside source (Caesar’s anonymous scribes- the equivalent of contemporary AI) or done through a computer-based language model (AI), the result may be performed by someone or something undisclosed. The artificiality of the matter at hand lies in the false claim of authorship.  A fraudulent presentation should be known and defined by what it is - a fraudulent presentation - not by its process.

_David Ganje is an attorney who practices natural resources, environmental and commercial law. His website is_ [_lexenergy.net_](https://linkprotect.cudasvc.com/url?a=http%3a%2f%2flexenergy.net%2f&c=E,1,xMYrSlOmVAgWoF7zCXxowh-vd__56TfAWreyS6DvcgtZ0s2NUDH1TUEqcKzfK2WCyR72SVebh2M4jpH83zHYopVrbotM1jA6jB59uNGh&typo=1)

## [South Dakota’s GOP Sen. Thune will have a much different job next year.](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/south-dakota-republican-senator-john-thune-will-have-a-much-different-job-next-year)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-14, updated 2026-11-27

For the past two years our GOP Sen. John Thune has been senate majority leader. He has consistently bowed to the wishes of President Trump, never offering a discouraging word.

No Republican stood up to Trump.

That is one reason for the landslide election of enough non-Republican Senators to put the GOP in the minority in both houses of congress. Thune has spent the last two years attacking Democrats nearly every day. He quit working for ‘We, The People,’ several years ago. He has not protected us from Trump’s actions and words.

It is surprising that the Republican caucus selected him to be minority leader. Apparently, no other Republican wanted the job, kind of like nobody wants the helm when the ship is sinking.

In the House of Representatives, Republicans have selected Jim Jordan from Ohio to lead the Republican minority. In contrast to the low-keyed, circumspect Thune, Jordan is known for being a brash, in-your-face kind of guy.

If may be fun to watch Jordan and Thune as they try to remain relevant while leading a significantly diminished Republican presence in congress.

I wonder how much political protection they will provide lame duck Trump.

As of this writing, Democrats have not selected their leaders. It appears there is not much public support for minority leader Chuck Schumer in the Senate and minority leader Hakeem Jeffries in the House.

We, the people have spoken. Loudly. Are the people in Washington listening?

I am cautiously optimistic. I want our country to be decent again. I want our leaders to remember their oaths to the country, not their party.

The first 100 days of 2027 will be interesting.

_Rick Knobe is a former mayor of Sioux Falls and a long-time radio talk show host, now retired._

_Photo: public domain,_ [_wikimedia commons_](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:President_Donald_Trump_and_Melania_Trump_with_Senator_John_Thune.jpg)

## [Trump administration blames ‘overloaded’ systems for ground stop after FAA lets flights resume](https://federalnewsnetwork.com/technology-main/2026/11/flights-are-halted-at-airports-across-the-united-states-due-to-unspecified-extraordinary-circumstances-faa-says/)

via Federal News Network · 2026-11-14, updated 2026-11-17

The Federal Aviation Administration says airplane departures can resume after the agency lifted a nationwide ground stop caused by communications problems. The FAA had grounded all flights nationwide Friday afternoon, causing hundreds of flights to be diverted. Officials cited “extraordinary circumstances” caused by communications problems at regional air traffic control centers. Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin told reporters at the White House that the introduction of some new technology into an old air traffic control system overwhelmed it. The nationwide ground stop was the first since January 2023, when a key FAA computer system malfunctioned, and the second since the aftermath of the 9/11 attacks.

## [The Fed vs. AI inflation](https://www.marketplace.org/episode/2026/11/13/the-fed-vs-ai-inflation)

via Market Place · 2026-11-13

Ah, the duality of AI. Spending on the technology is boosting economic growth, yes, but it’s also driving up inflation. (In case you were too distracted by Jensen-Huang-enfreude, wholesale inflation was up 6.5% YoY in October.) What’s the Fed — which hasn’t met its 2% inflation goal in over five years — to do? In this episode, how the central bank might be thinking about AI ahead of its next meeting. Plus: Nvidia execs are likely getting very, very prepared for next week’s earnings call and private equity gets its paws on more and more food and beverage chains.

This morning, we learned that the producer price index rose by 0.3% in October, up 6.5% year over year. At this point, the Fed's 2% inflation target feels far away, and wage gains haven't kept up with price increases for some seven months now. We discuss all that, then explain a Supreme Court case centered on the power of government agencies.

## [Bucking the national trend, S.D. Democrats continue to flounder](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/bucking-the-national-trend-south-dakota-democrats-continue-to-flounder)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-13, updated 2026-11-27

While Democrats swept the Midterm elections nationally, with a "tsunami" that reached Texas, Kansas, South Carolina and possibly Mississippi, in South Dakota the [Republicans scored yet another sweep](https://electionresults.sd.gov/), winning all the statewide races by somewhere between 58% (Heather Baxter for Secretary of State) and 64% (Marty Jackley for Congress) of the total vote.

South Dakota Democrats did gain legislative seats, moving from just 8 out of 105 legislators all the way up to 12 out of 105 legislators, but these gains were entirely in the Sioux Falls area. Even the districts representing our nine Native American reservations, which were designed for Democratic wins, were swept by Republican candidates, with the lone exception of Eric Emery, who will continue to represent the Rosebud reservation.

Why did South Dakota buck the national trend and go solidly Republican at a time when Donald Trump inspired so many Democratic victories? Have South Dakota Democrats been down so long that we can no longer pick ourselves up?

It's a fact that no South Dakota Democrat has won a statewide race since 2008, when Sen. Tim Johnson and Rep. Stephanie Herseth Sandlin both won landslide re-elections. That was also the year that Barack Obama won the Presidency, earning almost 45% of the vote in South Dakota and carrying 14 of our 66 counties, including Minnehaha and Brown.

In the years that followed, rural America turned sharply away from Obama and the Democrats and helped elect Donald Trump at the end of Obama's Presidency. As America has become totally polarized between the major urban areas, which are increasingly Democratic, and the small cities and rural areas which are heavily Republican, South Dakota has clearly identified as a red state. While Kansas just elected its first Democratic Senator since 1932, South Dakota has not elected a Democratic Governor since 1974.

We used to pride ourselves on having some of the highest voter turnouts in America. This month, only 49% of South Dakota voters bothered to go to the polls. As many voters perceived that none of our major races were actually competitive, the low turnout became a self-fulfilling prophecy. One party dominance is not good for democracy.

As we move toward 2028, when America will elect a new President, what can we do to start to rebuild a genuine two-party system in South Dakota? This state was very competitive politically from the late 1950's into the early 2000's.

Many of our rural communities actually leaned Democratic in those years. George McGovern single-handedly built a strong, competitive Democratic Party, which elected Jim Abourezk, Dick Kneip, Tom Daschle, Tim Johnson and McGovern himself. As the Trump Presidency continues to flounder, South Dakota Democrats need to return to serious grassroots organizing.

This needs to expand beyond the Sioux Falls metro area to our small cities, small towns and rural areas. South Dakotans need to be convinced that their votes really might make a difference. Then we may be able to get out of our one-party rut.

_Jay Davis is a retired Rapid City attorney_

## [New Democratic congress should be progressive, not retributive](https://www.sdstandardnow.com/home/which-democratic-congress-will-emerge)

via South Dakota Standard · 2026-11-12, updated 2026-11-27

The November 3 election gave the Democratic Party in Congress a strong majority in both houses. As a result, they have the ability to pass anything they want. That majority came about because large numbers of independents and moderate republicans were upset and disappointed in the actions of president Trump. The war, the economy, the deficits and the excesses of ICE were major factors in the defection resulting in those “swing” voters voting for democrats in the election. They voted against those items and simply want them to end and expect Democrats to do that.

Many or most democrats voted because they are angry at the vicious and vindictive way Trump has attacked liberals, immigrants and institutions. In addition to the “swing” items, many or most democrats voted for revenge. They want Congress to be as vindictive in return as Trump was to them.

The democratic response in Congress can be negative or positive. If they choose to respond negatively and to cater to the democratic base and be vindictive, they can impeach, investigate and attack this administration, and there is more than enough reason to do so.

However, impeaching Trump for the umpteenth time accomplishes nothing. There aren’t votes to convict and remove him from office and worse yet, impeachment could generate sympathy for Trump among the moderates that voted against him.He will not be on the ballot in 2028 and those moderates need a new reason to continue to vote democratic.

Even if they could get the Republican votes to remove him, it would allow JD Vance to have the advantage of incumbency in the next election. They can spend lots of time investigating the myriad of questionable and [legally dubious](https://campaignlegal.org/CanTrumpDoThat) actions of this administration.

But that is not what the swing voters voted for. They want it to discontinue in the future, but the past is past and they only want a better future. A negative Democratic congress risks losing their gains among swing voters. In the short term it would feel good, but the long term loss of those voters would be costly.

A positive response would be to enact legislation that is popular among the majority of Americans rather than attacks that only appeal to the base. There are many positive bills that the congress could pass in the next budget that would be popular among both democrats and swing voters. Among them:

_Deficit reduction_. Clinton left a budget that did not require borrowing. The congress could reverse the Bush and Trump tax cuts and return to the balanced budget tax rates of Clinton. And a spending plan within those limits. Increase taxes on billionaires. In addition to the Clinton tax rates, a modest increase in tax rates on the highest income levels would be popular and help balance the budget.

_Reducing or eliminating tax subsidies to corporations_. Not necessary to raise tax rates, but subsidies such as oil companies that made three times as much profit during the war as they did the year previously.

_Defunding ICE_. Make major reductions in ICE while requiring professional behavior. The training and leadership has been abysmal and Congress should require improvements.

_Defunding the war_. Trump has ramped up the military to be an offensive weapon of war on other countries, rather than a defense of America first. The military needed to defend America is vastly smaller that one that wages war. We don’t need more veterans of FOREIGN wars.

_Restoring fairness to all Americans_. Restore the university research that has maintained American technological and intellectual leadership in the world and been the solid base for economic growth.

_Limit the ability to levy tariffs_ which are a sales tax on Americans. Tariffs were the single largest tax increase in history. Some tariffs are necessary or at least useful.

_Restore military aid to Ukraine, allowing them to resist tyranny_.

These initiatives do two things that are advantageous to Democrats. They show that the Democratic Party can not only _complain_, but can _govern_. That is critical. They also put Donald Trump in an impossible position. These items are popular, but Trump would not want Democrats to be able to take credit for them. However, vetoing them would continue to alienate swing voters. Worse yet, vetoing a budget would shut down the country.

Trump has had [four government shutdowns](https://www.statista.com/chart/12624/duration-of-government-shutdowns/?srsltid=AYCX9vK9UIK9q2KO-CsJCh0MniRI4899NDoRdVi4s8IQ6ajhogMUQNy4) and none have been beneficial to him. The 2018 shutdown was particularly humiliating. He can’t afford that. It is the positive way to generate negative results for Trump and pave the way for future victories.

The Democratic Congress will have to demonstrate a certain amount of vindictiveness to placate the democratic base. And there should be consequences for the outrageous acts of this administration.

But it should not be the focus. Those investigations should not be where Democrats spend their media attention. The media will cover it extensively because it is news. The Democratic congress should focus its attention on showing America the positive things they believe in and are working on, not just because it is good for America, but because promoting positive bills will demonstrate the ability to govern and can maintain the coalition that they will need in future elections.

_John Cunningham of Sioux Falls has spent his career in local government finance, including in Fulton County, Ga., and Atlanta. He has a master of public administration from Harvard and has done consultant work in four foreign countries_
